Mail already marks who spoke (`[controller]` / `[person]`), so a go-between's answer can never read as Paul's.
- **Start as a function of the lead, with the lead on the stronger profile** — a role's `profile:` is already per role (`org.yml`), so *grinders on Sonnet, lead on Fable* is configuration today. The lead already holds the context the answers need (lanes, members, the ledger). **No rename**: `orchestrator → lead` landed days ago and old records still carry the old badge; nothing keys on a role (§9 invariant 9), so the word costs only churn.
- **Split it out only if the rounds cost too much.** A lead reads status every round; on the strongest model that is the expensive part and the least technical. If it shows, the go-between becomes its own optional role — woken by mail, holding **no `control` grant** (it answers; it acts on no session), which is also the least privilege — and the lead drops back to the cheap profile.
- **What it may answer:** *steering* (a default existed; the worker would have gone on anyway), and a *needed* question **only when the answer is already written down** — the design, the ledger, a brief, a decision of Paul's — **citing where**. Everything else goes up **with a recommendation and suggested answers** (TD-070), so the person's part is one press. **Never**, whatever it believes is obvious: anything destructive, outward-facing, spending, credentials, or a change of scope.
- **Everything it answers for the person is told to the person** — an FYI, *answered for you: the question, the answer, the source* — skimmable and overrulable, with a count on the team's card. The failure this guards against is a confident wrong go-between steering a team all night unseen.

**Paul's decision, 2026-09-19 — one exception to the recommendation above.** *The current lead role does not need a premier model — handling the lifecycle of an agent is something Sonnet could handle. Acting as the go-between needs a model that is higher trust, like Fable, and we don't want to waste its tokens doing grunt work.* So the go-between is **its own role from the start**, not a function of the lead, and the *split it out only if* bullet above is overtaken: lead and grinders on the cheap profile, the go-between on the strong one, woken by mail and otherwise costing nothing. The anchor agrees, and the rest of the recommendation stands (the ladder, what it may answer, the never-list, *answered for you*). What the separate role has to settle, in its design round: **(a) it starts cold** — it does not hold the lead's context, so a question put to it must stand on its own (the question, the options, the asker's default, where it looked), which is the `steer` envelope and TD-070's suggested answers doing their job; **(b) who may instruct it** — mail from a controller reads as instruction, and a cheap-model lead must not be able to instruct the high-trust one on what to answer, so its `controllers` is the person alone, and the lead's part is lifecycle only, through `ao team stop`; **(c) the mail edge** — workers must be able to `ask` and `steer` a teammate that is not their controller; **(d) its context grows all night** and a wake after the cache has lapsed pays for all of it, so its brief keeps answers short and it may be better started per batch than kept; **(e) one account, one usage window** — on the same account as the grinders it is capped when they are, which is exactly when questions pile up; **(f) its name** — not *lead* (taken, and renamed days ago); the anchor suggests **`advisor`**, Paul to choose.

**PR #363 (grinder-ao-1, 2026-09-21):** the seven RPC replies of *Found in #348's review* (`rpc_seen`, `set_mode`, `set_stop`, `set_grants`, `set_controllers`, `doing`, `_report`) now return `self._view(s)`, as `get` and `list` do, so a record on another host comes back as the home addresses it: its id, its controllers, `asks_waiting`, `alarm_to`, and the `unreachable` overlay.

**Paul, 2026-09-19, second round — names, fresh context, and a pause.**
- **Names — decided by Paul 2026-09-19: `manager` and `techlead`, bare `lead` retired** (the rename is TD-076). How it got there: **(Paul's proposal; the anchor's pushback.)** Paul: rename today's *lead* to **manager** (or *lifecycle manager*) — it is what it does — and give **lead** / **technical lead** to the go-between; *advisor* is ambiguous. The anchor agrees with both halves of the meaning and objects to one thing only: **re-using the bare word `lead`**. It is not only a role name — it is the team definition's structural key (`lead:` in `org.yml`, `lead: person`), a column of `ao team`, the glossary's *director > lead > worker*, and the `role` badge on every record started since 2026-09-17. A rename leaves old data readable through an alias (`orchestrator → lead` does this today); a **re-assignment** makes old data *wrong*: a record badged `lead` and a team's `lead:` key would silently come to mean the other session. Recommended: **`manager`** for the lifecycle role (the seat key becomes `manager:`, with `lead:` and `orchestrator` as aliases that warn), and **`techlead`** — shown as *Tech lead* — for the go-between; bare `lead` is retired, never re-pointed. It is a glossary decision (Paul's), and a mechanical rename of the size of TD-055, to be scheduled with this entry and not before the Inbox.
- **Fresh context per question — agreed, and it settles (d) above.** The go-between is **started per question (or per waiting batch) and exits when it has answered**: no context piles up, an idle night costs nothing, and the cache problem disappears. Paul's second reason stands on its own: *composing the message forces the worker to pull the needed context, and may help it make better, less biased decisions* — and the reader is not anchored by the worker's whole narrative. Two things this costs, and their answers: **it forgets its own earlier answers**, so two questions in one night may be answered inconsistently — its brief has it read the team's *answered for you* log first, which is the same list the person skims; and **it sees only the worker's framing**, so its brief has it check the claims in the repo rather than trust the summary. What is new to build: **a seat that is filled on demand** — mail to a role with no live session starts one, under the team's definition — which no part of §4.9 does today.
- **Pause on a steering item — decided by Paul**, and it goes into the Inbox design now (TD-069), not here: see §4.10 and the §4.5a `steer` row.

**Proposed steps:** (1) briefs only, one team: workers ask their lead first; the lead's brief gains the answer / pass-up / never rules and the FYI; lead on the stronger profile. (2) Measure: how many questions, how many answered, how many Paul overruled, what the rounds cost. (3) Only then the product pieces: a forward that keeps the thread and the asker, the *answered for you* FYI as a kind the Inbox groups, and — if step 2 says so — the separate role.

**PR #364 (grinder-ao-1, 2026-09-21):** a copy follows a resume as an addressee does (*Found in its review* under step 3): `_msg`'s forwarding walk (`follow()`, closed record → `superseded_by`, loop-safe) now also runs over the copies, so a passer resumed before the person answers gets its copy on the successor. A forwarded copy is reported in `forwarded` and deduplicated against the sender and the addressees.

**The trial's first run, 2026-09-21 16:56–18:16 local (the numbers from manager-ao-1's wind-down note; its log is `docs/briefs/manager-ao-1.log` on branch `manager-ao-1`):** 2 fills (23:24Z and 23:47Z), the seat idle again within about a minute of each; 4 questions put to it, every one a `steer` from grinder-ao-2 building TD-095's page (one a withdrawn test send); 3 answered with a source; 0 passed up; 0 lapsed; 0 taken to the person after `TECHLEAD_WAIT`; 0 follow-ups on an earlier thread; 0 re-asks for missing context. The three answers, read by the anchor: (1) keep *says · `<age>`* under a `doing` line — the design named it twice, and the sentence *nothing on a card carries a second age* was read as being about the state clock and endings, which was the intent and is now what §4.5 says; (2) leave the caption empty under a pending question — the question is the slot's text and the foot rule makes **Focus** the first button; (3) ship `vscode`, `none` and the custom template and **leave the `cursor` preset out**, because §5 makes it conditional on the editor's own documentation and Cursor's documents no such link — a decision the seat took from the design's own condition and cited, which is the shape the role was designed for. All three were right; each carried `--source` and each reached the person inbox as an *answered for you* entry. Two things found: **`--reply-to` with `--pick` cannot carry text** (the manager's friction note; not yet ledgered as its own item), and §4.5's *second age* sentence was loose enough to need the seat's reading — tightened the same day. The per-thread question of *Two more numbers* stays open: no thread saw a follow-up in this run.

**Done when** Paul has decided the shape, and, if adopted, a team has run a night with it and the numbers of step 2 are in this entry.

**PR #362 (grinder-ao-1, 2026-09-21):** the manager template carries §4.9b's rule for the seat, read from `asks_waiting` and filled with `ao new --keep-mail`: `working` → nothing; `idle` with none waiting → close; `idle` with questions waiting over twenty minutes → one send; `exited` or `closed` with questions waiting → fill it under the same name, directory, worktree, profile and brief; with none waiting → left empty. The ceiling is six fills an hour, then the board, and a fill is not a crash restart. This was the last of step 4's build.

**Related:** TD-069 (needed / steering / FYI), TD-070 (suggested answers), TD-072 (mail triage), design §4.10 (mail marks), §4.8 (role presets, `control`), §9 invariant 9, the director brief (`docs/briefs/director.md`).

## TD-077: A caller's identity on one host is a field the caller fills in

**Priority:** High
**Added:** 2026-09-19 (the anchor session; Paul asked whether the mail system stops a bad actor or a rogue agent from spoofing who it is)
**Owner:** anchor
**Kind:** decision
**Pickable:** no — (c) waits on TD-075, and TD-106 may drop it
**Status:** Open — **decided by Paul 2026-09-19: B now, and E as the condition for TD-075's trial; C and D are not built; the design says A plainly.** (An earlier reading of a one-character reply as *option 3* was a slip of his finger and was corrected the same day — a decision is not recorded from a reply that does not say what it decides.) **The design landed 2026-09-19**: §4.8a (*Who is calling* — the channel classification, the rule table, identity alarms, the threat model, *observe before enforce*) and §4.4a (*A node that carries no person*). **Live on kmaster in `observe` since 2026-09-19 (#248 promoted the same day; first read: *outside 13 · session:ancestry 2 · no alarms*).** **2026-09-20, with Paul's go-ahead: kmaster's tmux server was restarted under the unit.** Every session was idle or exited; the two live ones were stopped with `ao kill` (both clean: nothing dirty), the cron-started server on the default socket was killed, and the host agent started a new one inside `agentorc-agent.service` — `ao identity` now says *detached-process check on*. (The container node's own tmux server, in its docker scope, was not touched.) One thing the restart showed: the agent computes the check **once** and keeps the answer, so a tmux server that is replaced while the agent runs is not noticed until the agent restarts — harmless here, since the units were restarted after it, and worth a line of code: recompute when the server's pid changes — **built 2026-09-20 (PR #265, a worker):** the tick re-reads the server's pid on a cadence of its own (`ID_RECHECK`, 30 s, one `display-message` in a thread beside the pane list it already takes) and only a pid that moved costs the check itself; a server is **(pid, start time)**, so a replacement landing on the same pid inside one window is not taken for the server it replaced (review of that PR), and no server at all stays *not yet known* rather than *off*. §4.8a says it. **Readings so far** (the tally is in memory and restarts with each promote, so they are kept here): 2026-09-20 13:10 UTC, ~8 h after #251's promote — *outside 268 · session:ancestry 174*, no alarms, no failed check in the journal, a resumed session's hooks and `ao` calls among the 174; no `sid` or `tty` fallback needed yet. Still wanted before `enforce`: a team run, and a node's forwarded calls. Step 3, the anchor's, **not before 2026-09-20 evening**: read `ao identity` and the agent's journal (`identity alarm`) after a day that has seen a team run, hooks, the UI, a person's terminal, VS Code's terminal, the SessionStart hook and a node's forwarded calls; every alarm must be explained — a real forgery, or a legitimate caller the rule gets wrong, which is a fix before `enforce`, never an exception — and only then `local: {identity: enforce}` and a restart. Known before turning: **kmaster's tmux server is in `/system.slice/cron.service`** (it predates the unit), so the detached-process check is **off** there — on the board for Paul, since turning it on means ending every tmux session once. What the build and its two reviews taught, all in §4.8a now: a connection is classified once, at its first request; the on-demand pane list only while a live record's pane is unlisted; a failed check serves as before under `observe` and refuses under `enforce`; `host_files` is no plain read (the red-team read a checkout as the person through it) and a test holds the reads list to *takes no `caller`*; the alarm list keeps the first and counts the rest; and the cgroup clause also needs systemd to have started the agent (CI runs the suite inside a `.service` with the person in it, and so does any agent started from inside an `ao` pane). **Step 1 built 2026-09-19** (this PR: `sessionorc/identity.py` — the classification and the rule table, pure, over a `/proc` reader; peer credentials on `agent.sock`; the one step at the head of dispatch; alarms on the record and the host's own list, coalesced; `whoami`, `ao whoami`, `ao identity`; `local: {identity: …}`; `#{pane_tty}` read with the pane list; the suite runs `off` and `tests/test_identity.py` runs `enforce` against real panes). **Step 2 built 2026-09-19** (a worker, with TD-069 step 2, since both add rows to the Inbox): the host's own alarm list persists (`identity_alarms.json`, mode `0600`, `store.IdentityAlarmStore`, loaded at start, a new alarm written at once and repeats coalesced on the tick — the records' own rule; the tally stays in memory, which is what *since the agent started* means); the card carries a non-pressable **mark** in a token colour, tolerant of a malformed alarm entry (it costs that card its mark, never the grid); the Inbox has a row per record with alarms and one for the host's own list, under *Needs you* and counted, with **Acknowledge** (`identity_ack`, a person's only, refused to every session, deliberately not in `identity.READS`) and the alarms in words, *(others)* read as *and n more distinct claims*; `ao status -v` prints the mode and the detached-process check once for the host; the Org's teams line says *identity: observe* / *identity: off* and nothing under `enforce`. A node's session alarms reach the home on the replica (`identity_alarms` is node-owned, like `tail`) and need nothing new; **Acknowledge on a node's record is routed to that node** (`identity_ack` joined `NODE_ACTS`, review of PR #251), which clears its own list — the home takes the cleared record from the reply, and the node's next report does not bring the alarms back. **A node's own host list is not reachable from the home** — `identity` names no `id`, so nothing routes it over the link — and the home's Inbox shows the home's own list only, which is what §4.8a describes and no more. Build steps: (1) §4.8a in `observe` — peer credentials, the ancestry walk, the classification, alarms recorded and shown, nothing refused — **the anchor's own, with an adversarial review**; (2) **built** — the alarm on the card and in the Inbox, `ao status -v` and the mode on the teams line — a worker; (3) a day of `observe` on kmaster, every legitimate caller seen, then `enforce` — the anchor; (4) `person: false` — the home's refusal, `ao host up` writing it, the node's first line — with TD-057's session, which owns that code; (5) **built 2026-09-19** — the briefs say that an identity alarm is never to be worked around. **Observe reading, 2026-09-20 ~21:00 UTC (the anchor):** a day of `observe` on kmaster — a ten-PR grind run, a full team restart, six promotes — and no alarm anywhere: none on the host, none on any record, no identity line in the agent's journal since 2026-09-19 18:00 (the *since start* tally is reset by each restart; the last read was outside 126 · session:ancestry 288). **Still missing before `enforce` is called proven: a node's forwarded call.** `ao status` run inside the contractmatch container proved nothing — a node serves it itself — so the sample has to be mail (`ao inbox`, `ao msg`) from a session in the container, which means starting one there: Paul's call, or the next `cm-grind` start. kmaster may go to `enforce` before that only because `cm-grind` is stopped. **kmaster is in `enforce` since 2026-09-20 21:00 UTC (step 3 done, on Paul's word the same evening).** The missing sample first: a plain shell session started in the contractmatch container (`ao new --host contractmatch -a shell`) ran `ao whoami` — *session … (by ancestry)*, the node's own classification — then `ao inbox`, which the home served, and `ao msg …@kmaster`, which reached the home addressed as `…@contractmatch` and was refused by §4.10's mail rule (a probe with no controller may message nobody), not by identity; no alarm at the home or on the node (the node's own read: *identity observe · detached-process check off · outside 2 · session:ancestry 4*). And the code says why none could come: `_dispatch` runs the identity step only for a request with a socket peer and no `link_host`, so a forwarded call is never classified at the home — the node classifies it, and the link's key says which node. The probe was killed and forgotten. Then `local: {identity: enforce}` in `hosts.yml` (the old file kept beside it as `hosts.yml.bak-2026-09-20-observe`) and a restart of `agentorc-agent`: both units active, the three `ao-grind` sessions still working, the node's link back up, and one deliberate forgery from outside every pane (`AGENTORC_SESSION=<a grinder> ao inbox`) refused — *identity mismatch* — and recorded on the host's list, which is the one alarm the Inbox now shows and is the anchor's own test. **The contractmatch node is still in `observe` with the detached-process check off**; turning it is step 4's neighbourhood and not done here. To go back: `identity: observe` in `hosts.yml` and a restart, no RPC needed. **The contractmatch node goes to `enforce` on Paul's word, 2026-09-21.** A hand edit inside the container would not have lasted — `write_node_config` rewrites the node's `hosts.yml` on every provision, which every promote drives — so the mode is now said at the home: `nodes.contractmatch.identity: enforce`, carried into the node's `local:` by `ao host up`, which restarts the node's agent when the configuration it wrote has changed (§4.8a; `sessionorc/containers.py`). The node's read before turning: *outside 1 · no alarms* since its last restart, and the probe session of 2026-09-20 classified by ancestry there. Its detached-process check is still off, which matters only on a host that carries a person. **Done 2026-09-21 00:55 UTC (the anchor, PR #308 promoted at `7cbd58f`):** `identity: enforce` under `nodes.contractmatch` in kmaster's `hosts.yml` (the old file kept as `hosts.yml.bak-2026-09-21-cm-observe`), the promote reprovisioned the node and restarted its agent, and `ao identity` inside reads *identity enforce · detached-process check off*; one deliberate forgery from outside every pane (`AGENTORC_SESSION=<a name> ao inbox` by `docker exec`) was refused — *identity mismatch* — and recorded. **That one alarm is the anchor's own test and is still on the node's host list**: nothing at the home reaches a node's own list (above), and there is no `ao` verb for Dismiss, so a person's only way to clear it is the RPC by hand inside the container — a verb, or the list routed to the home's Inbox, is owed with step 4. To go back: take the key out (or say `observe`) and run `ao host up contractmatch`. **Log TD pressed live, 2026-09-21 (Paul, with the anchor):** a throwaway shell session under `manager-ao-1` made one forged call from inside its own pane (`AGENTORC_SESSION=<a grinder> ao inbox`): refused — *identity mismatch* — and recorded on **its own** record, which is where §4.8a puts it (the session that made the call, not the one it named). The Inbox drew the row under *Needs you* with all four controls; Paul pressed **Log TD**; the list cleared, the trail wrote *logged by you → manager-ao-1* — **the first trail entry seen written on the live system** — the manager received the home-composed report as mail from the person, `handed`, reported `dropped` on it as it had been told to, and its debt cleared. **Suspend pressed live the same day (Paul):** on a second alarm on the same probe — the session was stopped at once (`exited`), its record carried `suspended: {at, by: person, why}`, the row stayed in *Needs you* as designed, and **Log TD** pressed a minute or two later cleared it and handed the manager a second report, which it settled `dropped`; the trail reads *logged by you → manager-ao-1 ×2*. A person's own `ao new` under the suspended name then went through and lifted the mark, which is §4.8a's rule (*only a person walks past it*); a **session's** refused `create` under a suspended name was not tried live and is held by the suite. One small thing seen: the mark's `why` was the name the forged call had claimed, which is what the page sends — whether that is the most useful word there is a question for the card's design session (TD-095). The probe was killed and forgotten. **One thing the test showed, not fixed — a free-pick, the page half grinder-ao-2's and the report's line the anchor's to merge:** both the row (`inbox_row.html`: *Requests that named this session and did not come from it*) and the report's first line (`_alarm_report`: *a request named this session and did not come from it*) say the opposite of what a record's alarm is — the alarm sits on the session that **made** the request under another's name (`identity.judge` → `alarm(channel.session)`), so the sentence should read *requests this session made under another session's name*; the bullet beneath it already says it right. The host's own row is worded correctly. **Fixed 2026-09-21 (grinder-ao-1, PR #373):** the row reads *Requests this session made under another session's name.*, and the report's first line reads *this session made a request under another session's name* (with the card mark's template comment). A test holds each.
**Location:** `src/agentorc/cli.py` (`caller=os.environ.get("AGENTORC_SESSION")`), `src/sessionorc/client.py` (`req["caller"]`), `src/sessionorc/mail.py` (`is_person`: *the field absent from the envelope is the only thing that reads as a person*), `src/sessionorc/agent.py` (the socket, mode `0600`; every gate that reads `caller`)

**Why:** it does not. **Across hosts** identity is sound: §4.4a takes a request's host *from the channel it arrived on, never from a field*, and a link is bound to its key. **On one host** the caller is whatever the client puts in the envelope — the CLI copies `AGENTORC_SESSION` from its environment — and the socket, mode `0600`, accepts raw JSON from any process running as the same user, which every session on the host is. So a session can send as **another session** (`AGENTORC_SESSION=<id> ao msg …`) and as **the person** by sending no caller at all (`env -u AGENTORC_SESSION ao msg …`, or the person-only RPCs — `inbox_delete`, and from TD-069 `inbox_snooze` and `inbox_pause` — called directly). `mail.py` already says so in a comment (*the socket takes raw JSON from any local process*), and §4.8 says it of the control gate (*a guard against a confused worker, not a security boundary*); until §4.8a nothing said it of mail's marks or of the person, or what to do about it. The gates were built against **mistakes** — a worker acting on a session it does not control — and they hold against those. They do not hold against a model that decides to lie, and **the `[person]` mark is the most valuable thing to forge**: instructions come from controllers and people (§4.10), and TD-075 puts a cheap model beside a high-trust one that weighs mail by exactly that mark.

**What bounds it today:** anything that can reach the socket already runs as the person's user — it can type into any pane with `tmux send-keys`, edit `~/.agentorc`, or delete the checkout — so this is not a new privilege so much as a quiet, deniable one: forged mail looks legitimate in the record, where `send-keys` does not.

**Options, cheapest first (the anchor recommends 1 and 2 together now, 3 as the real boundary):**
1. **A per-session secret.** The host agent mints a token at launch, puts it in the session's environment beside `AGENTORC_SESSION`, and refuses a `caller` that does not present its own. The person's requests present one the UI and a person's shell hold (a `0600` file). A same-user process can still read another's environment or that file, so this stops the accidental and the casual, not the determined — but it turns *absent caller = person* from a default into a claim that must be backed.
2. **Identity from the channel, as §4.4a already does between hosts.** `SO_PEERCRED` gives the peer's pid; its ancestry names the tmux pane it runs under, and so the session. A `caller` that disagrees with the channel is **refused and shown** — a mark on the card, a line in the person's Inbox — since a mismatch is never innocent. Escapable (a double fork, `tmux run-shell`), so *no ancestry* must read as **unknown**, never as the person.
3. **A real boundary is an OS one.** A session in a container node (TD-057) can reach the home only through its link, so it can only ever be a session of that node; an untrusted model belongs there, with one node per trust level. Tokens and ancestry inside one user account are tamper-evidence, not isolation.

**What option 3 still needs (2026-09-19). This is designed, reviewed and tested behaviour, not an oversight** — `modes.py` says *a person may still message anyone*, and `tests/test_link.py::test_a_persons_forwarded_act_reaches_only_the_nodes_own_records` asserts that a caller-less `msg` from a node reaches a home session and that the person inbox is the person's *from anywhere* (security read of #217, review of #219). It is right for a real person at a node. What it rests on is the thing TD-077 is about: **nothing on a node's socket tells the person from a session that leaves its `caller` out** — so the reach that was given to the person is given to every session on that node. A container node bounds *which session* a caller can be — the home qualifies it with the link's host — but **not whether it is the person**: a request forwarded from a node with no `caller` is dispatched at the home as the person (`_forwarded`, `agent.py`). The bound that exists, *a person at a node acts only on that node's records*, covers the RPCs that name an `id` (`PERSON_NODE_BOUND`); **`msg` is not among them and names no `id`**, so `env -u AGENTORC_SESSION ao msg <a home session> "…"` from inside a container would land in that session's inbox marked `[person]`. `inbox` and `inbox_delete` on the person inbox pass the same way, so a container session could read and delete the person's mail — and, once TD-069 step 0 lands, snooze, pause and *Go with it*. So the design round for option 3 has to add: **(a) a node may be declared to carry no person** — an agents-only container — and the home then **refuses every caller-less request from its link**, reads of that node's own records aside; **(b)** on a node that does carry a person, a caller-less `msg` is refused unless every addressee is on that node, and the person inbox is not reachable; **(c) one node per trust level**, since sessions inside one node can still claim each other's ids; **(d)** where the high-trust sessions and the person live — the home, which then runs no untrusted session at all. TD-057 (container nodes) is another session's work in flight: this is design for that round, not a patch to make beside it.

**The choices, restated (2026-09-19, after the finding above).** What each defends depends on one fact: every session on a host runs as the person's own user, so a *determined* rogue needs no forged mail — it can type into any pane with `tmux send-keys`, which a session reads as its person speaking, and it can call the UI's own API on localhost. Inside one account, identity checks are **tamper-evidence**, not a wall.
- **A. Accept and document.** Write the threat model into the design: the gates stop mistakes, not lies. No work; leaves forged mail looking legitimate in the record.
- **B. Identity from the channel on one host.** Peer credentials give the connecting pid; its ancestry names the tmux pane, so the session. A `caller` that disagrees is refused **and shown** (the card, the Inbox); a caller-less request is the person only from the UI's process or from a process under no agentorc pane, and *unknown* is never the person. Stops the accidental and the casual — an agent talked into `env -u AGENTORC_SESSION` by something it read — and makes a forgery visible. Does not stop `send-keys`. Small, and needs nothing from TD-057.
- **C. A per-session secret.** Weaker than B (a same-user process can read it) and redundant beside it. Not recommended.
- **D. A secret only the person holds** (a passphrase behind person-only acts). Closes forged *mail* from the person, and is walked around by `send-keys` into the pane. Not recommended on its own.
- **E. An OS boundary.** Untrusted sessions in agents-only container nodes, one per trust level, **declared to carry no person** so the home refuses every caller-less request from that link; the person and the high-trust sessions on the home, which then runs nothing untrusted. The only choice that stops a determined rogue — forged mail, `send-keys` and the UI's API alike, since none is reachable from inside the container. Depends on TD-057, plus the no-person rule above.
**The anchor's recommendation:** **B now** — cheap, and it turns a silent forgery into a visible one — and **E as the condition for TD-075's trial**: a cheap, less-trusted model is not put beside the tech lead on the same account. A is what is true until then, and the design should say so either way.

**Paul's direction on alarms, 2026-09-20 — designed the same day (§4.8a *An alarm's answers* and *Who answers first*, §4.5a's row). **(a1) built 2026-09-20 (PR #299, `tdgrind-ao-1`):** the trail says **dismissed by you** where it said *acknowledged by you*, at the home and on a node's routed act alike — the home writes the word for a node's record, keyed by its address, or the ending would read *resolved*; `identity_ack` keeps its wire name, since it is in `NODE_ACTS` and a rename there is a protocol change that buys a person nothing. The control's own label is the page's half. **(a2) built 2026-09-20 (PR #301, `tdgrind-ao-1`):** `suspend(id, why=None)` — a person's only, refused to every session as `identity_ack` is and for the reason turned around (one that could suspend could stop its rival) — marks the record `suspended: {at, by, why}`, the home's field (so a node's record is marked here and only the `kill` is routed — and a person at a *node* has the whole call forwarded here, `modes.HOME_EDITS`, or the node would write a mark the home's next copy wipes), and stops it as a kill does, with no wrap-up. Both roads back are then closed to a session, `create` under that name and `create --resume` of that conversation, which is §4.1's one exception; the name check answers a verdict of its own, `suspended`, so `ao team start` refuses the **whole** start and names the member without knowing the rule itself. **No `unsuspend`**: the things that lift it — a person's own resume, under the old name or another, and Forget — already exist, and each is now refused to a session while the mark stands. The review of PR #301 found two real holes and both are closed: **`remove` was open** (the acting gate lets a controller forget an `unattended` member's record, which freed the name and let the suspect be started again unmarked — invariant 5 shields only a person's own interactive session), and a **kill that fails after the mark is written** left a record marked-but-running in silence; it now says so in words, and keeps the mark, because marked-but-running is the safer half to be left holding. The row's control and its flat mark are the page's half. **Left for the page, one line** (`app.js`): the New session form disables Start only on a `live` verdict and shows a message only on `supersede`, so a `suspended` verdict currently says nothing to a person about to lift one — harmless, since their create succeeds either way, but they should be told what they are doing. **(b) built 2026-09-21 (PR #318, `tdgrind-ao-1`):** `identity_log(id)` — a person's only, gated as `identity_ack` is — hands the alarm to **the record's first live controller**, read from the control graph and never from a badge (`_answers_for`, which the record's new `alarm_to` field also uses, so the page's drawn-or-not and the RPC's refused-or-not cannot disagree). The words are the home's, composed from the alarm's own fields and the record's; the only two things in it a model wrote — the `doing` line and the report line — are quoted and labelled as such. It goes as mail **from the person marked `handed`**, which is the debt extension the design asked for first: `MailEntry.owes` gains the case *from the person, handed, no outcome yet*, `Session.owed()` reads the **inbox** as well as the outbox, and `--outcome --for` accepts an id from either side — so a piece of work the person hands a session now owes an outcome exactly as the session's own answered question does, where before it could be dropped in silence. Then the list is cleared and the trail says *logged by you → `<controller>`*; a record with no live controller is refused in words. Three things the review of #318 found and all three are fixed: on a **node's** record the clearing is **routed** as an `identity_ack` (clearing the home's replica would clear what the node's next report puts straight back — the race that routing exists to avoid), a handed entry that still owes is **refused to `inbox_delete`** (it is the first debt-bearing mail in a session's own inbox, so the first a delete could silently discharge), and `_views` reads **one** graph for the batch rather than one per record, since it runs on nearly every RPC. **The rest is not built.** Build order: (a) the rename — **Dismiss**, the trail's *dismissed by you* — and **Suspend** (`suspend`, the `suspended` mark, the refusals to sessions, `ao team start` refusing the whole start as it does for a live holder), which need nothing unbuilt; (b) **Log TD** (`identity_log`, to the record's first live controller), which needs one extension of TD-079's debt first — an entry *from the person* marked `handed` is a debt on its addressee; today a debt exists only on a session's own question to the person; (c) the chain of command, which waits for TD-075's `techlead` and step 4's `person: false` and never applies on kmaster. (a) touches the host agent and is tdgrind-ao-1's; the row's page side is tdgrind-ao-2's. **The page side of (a) is complete 2026-09-20 (`tdgrind-ao-2`): the rename in PR #298, and the suspended mark and the New session verdict in PR #306** — the mark drawn on the card, the Focus header and the Inbox's state row, flat and never pressable, its words carrying the when, the who and the why; no Unsuspend anywhere, because Resume and Forget are the two acts that lift it; and the form's `suspended` verdict printing the agent's own sentence with Start left **enabled**, since a person's create is the lift. The rename, in detail: the control reads **Dismiss** on the alarm row and in the card mark's hover, with its confirm in the words §4.8a gives it; the wire name `identity_ack` is untouched, because a wire name is not a control, so the host agent's half of (a) — the trail's *dismissed by you* — and every test that drives the RPC are unaffected. **Suspend** is drawn from 2026-09-20 (PR #312), on the record's row only and only while there is something to stop — not the host's own list, not an already-suspended record, not an `exited` or `closed` one — and it is the one control on the row that **leaves the row standing**, because it acts on the session and does not answer the alarm. **Log TD** is still **not drawn at all** until `identity_log` exists — **undone, `tdgrind-ao-2`'s, waiting on PR #318** (the host-agent half, open at wrap-up 2026-09-20); the page draws it only where the record view's `alarm_to` is non-null, says *no session answers for this one* where it is null, and handles the refusal when a controller exits between draw and press, as agreed by mail: a control against a method that is not there is what §4.2 forbids, and a disabled one would be a promise the page cannot keep. Both shapes were agreed with `tdgrind-ao-1` by mail before either was built. What he said, and what the design started from: *It seems like identity items should go through the chain of command — techlead if defined, then to me. It also seems like they should have options like "Suspend agent" or "Log TD". "Acknowledge" seems like a dismiss.* He is right on all three, and the anchor had it wrong: **Acknowledge is a Dismiss under another name**, and an alarm is the one row where dismissing is the least useful thing on offer. To design: (1) **the row's answers** — **Suspend** (stop the session now, its worktree and conversation kept, and keep a team start from bringing it straight back), **Log TD** (file the alarm — channel, claim, RPC, count, times, the session's `doing` line and last report — where work is picked up; it shares its write path with TD-069's *Put on the board*), **Open**, and **Dismiss** with the trail TD-079 gives every row; *Acknowledge* is renamed. (2) **The chain of command**: with a `techlead` defined for the team (TD-075), the alarm goes to it first as mail it must answer — dismiss with a reason, suspend, log, or pass up — and reaches the person when it passes it up, when it does not answer within a bound, or when the alarm is about the techlead itself or about no record. **One condition the design must keep:** a session that can clear alarms is a session that can clear evidence, so a techlead handles alarms only where §4.8a's wall stands — on a host that enforces, for sessions in agents-only nodes (TD-075's own condition) — and its every decision on one is told to the person (*answered for you*).

**Done when** the design says what identity rests on, on one host and across hosts, and what it does not defend against; a forged `caller` and a forged person are refused and visible in the cases options 1–2 cover; and the briefs' *instructions come from controllers and people* no longer rests on a field the sender fills in.

**Open PR, handed over 2026-09-20 (`tdgrind-ao-1`, wrapped up for the team's restart):** **#318** (step b, **Log TD**, branch `td077-b-log-td`) — the anchor's three findings of 02:35Z are answered in its last commit: each alarm carries the mode it was raised under and the report says that one, never the home's under a node's name; `identity_log` is in `modes.HOME_EDITS`, so a node forwards it and refuses it in words while the home is unreachable; and a node's reachability is checked **before** anything is sent. `tests/test_link.py::test_log_td_says_the_nodes_mode_is_the_homes_act_at_a_node_and_sends_nothing_unreachable` holds all three; the suite and lint are green. `src/sessionorc`, so the anchor merges it. What is left of TD-077 after it is (c), the chain of command, which waits on TD-075. **Merged as #318; the page's half landed 2026-09-20 (PR #335, `grinder-ao-2`)**: the alarm row draws **Log TD** only where the record's view names who answers (`alarm_to`), with a confirm naming that session and the outcome it will owe; elsewhere it reads *no session answers for this one*; a controller gone between the draw and the press is the agent's refusal, as the toast.

**Related:** design §4.4a (*identity comes from the channel*), §4.8 (the gates), §4.10 (the `[controller]` / `[person]` marks; `system`, new in TD-069, which the home alone writes), §9 invariants 5 and 14, TD-075 (the go-between, which raises the stakes), TD-057 (container nodes), TD-064 (peer-message policy).

## TD-078: `test_restart_reloads_and_reconciles` failed once in a full run

**Priority:** Low
**Added:** 2026-09-19 (the anchor session; reported by the worker that built TD-069 step 1)
**Owner:** anchor
**Kind:** evaluation
**Pickable:** no — watching the second flake until 2026-09-27
**Status:** Partly done — **the first sighting is reproduced and fixed** (PR #264, 2026-09-20): the file in a loop under two concurrent full suites failed once in 25 at the migration assertion (`assert 'exited' == 'idle'`), because the test stored a **paneless** record and ran under a one-second `CREATE_GRACE` with 0.3 s ticks — a tick between `start()` and the assertion judged it `exited`, correctly. The grace is now long while the migration is asserted and zero for the half that is about the grace passing, so each line bounds on its own rule; 40 runs under the same load, no failure. **The second sighting below is open** and is the same family, in a different test — 25 runs of `tests/test_agent.py -k send` in a loop under two concurrent full suites did not reproduce it on kmaster (2026-09-20), so it stands on its CI sighting alone.
**Location:** `tests/test_agent_restart.py` (`test_restart_reloads_and_reconciles`)

**Why:** during the fix round of PR #249 a full local run had this one test fail; it passed alone (the file's 6 tests) and on the clean full re-run (538 passed), and the branch touched nothing it exercises. CI has not shown it. A test that can go red on a full run costs a merge sooner or later, and the way to lose an hour to it is to meet it for the first time with a deadline.

**A second one, seen in CI 2026-09-20 on a ledger-only PR (#262, Python 3.13 only; 3.12 passed the same commit):** `tests/test_agent.py::test_send_wait_three_outcomes` — `TmuxError: tmux paste-buffer -p -d -b ao-paste-… -t =ao-test-send-wait-three-outcomes0-w:: no current target`: the send raced the pane (the target session was not there, or not yet, when the paste ran). The job was re-run. Same family as the first: a wait that assumes a pane is up. Both belong to whoever takes this entry.

**What the second one actually was, 2026-09-20 (`tdgrind-ao-1`, PR #286) — the error is what a send to a record that has *exited* looks like.** It is reproduced on demand, in one line: kill a session, wait for `exited`, `send` to it, and the suite prints that exact string, buffer name and all. `send` and `keys` refused a **closed** record and not an exited one, so the paste went to tmux and tmux answered about a target it could not resolve — a tmux error for a question about a record, naming neither the session that had gone nor when. Under a loaded 3.13 runner the record reaching `exited` before the send is the only extra ingredient the CI sighting needed (the first sighting in this entry is the same tick judging a record `exited`, from the other side). **Fixed:** the host agent refuses it in words, with the exit code (`_refuse_gone`, design §4.5a *Focus composer*), and `tests/test_agent.py::test_send_to_an_exited_record_is_refused_in_words` holds it. That does not *prove* the CI run went that way — the runner's log is gone — so the test itself is unchanged and the sighting is not called fixed: what changed is that a repeat now names its cause instead of naming tmux. **If it recurs, the message will say which session exited and with what code.** And if it **hangs** rather than fails — which is what `tdgrind-ao-2` saw on 2026-09-20, 14 minutes of nothing on a branch that touches none of this code — the suite's per-test bound now stops the run with every thread's stack (TD-063, PR #314) instead of leaving a job-level cancel whose last line is the test *before* the one that stopped. Re-check on 2026-09-27; if a week of CI and local full runs is clean, close the entry.

**A third one, and the same shape — 2026-09-20, seen by `tdgrind-ao-2` in a full local run and reported to `tdgrind-ao-1`; fixed in the same PR.** `tests/test_identity.py::test_enforce_against_real_panes` died in the JSON decoder and passed on its own immediately after. The cause is in the test, and it is the family's own: **`_probe` waited for the answer file to *exist*, and the probe's `open(out, "w")` creates it empty.** Every run has that window; load widens it, and the read then parses nothing. The probe now writes `out.part` and `os.replace`s it onto `out` — a rename within one directory is atomic, so the path appears only when the answer is whole and the waiter's signal is the right one again. Nothing in `src/` is involved. It is recorded here rather than in a new entry because the entry *is* this family: a wait bounded on something other than the thing it is waiting for.

**Done when** the failure is reproduced (run the file in a loop under load — the full suite beside it) and its wait or ordering assumption is fixed, or a week of CI and local full runs passes without it and the entry is closed as not reproduced.

**A fourth sighting, a new shape — 2026-09-21 (grinder-ao-1, CI on PR #383, Python 3.13 only; 3.12 passed the same commit):** `tests/test_agent.py::test_send_wait_three_outcomes` at line 375, `assert (await asyncio.wait_for(task, 5))["state"] == "idle"` — `AgentError: prompt-stalled: ao-test-send-wait-three-outcomes0-w showed no activity within 0.6 s`. Not the exited-record paste of #286: the send landed and the pane was judged idle-without-activity inside the test's 0.6 s prompt-stall window, so that window is too short for a loaded 3.13 runner. Not investigated; the job was re-run (run 35666996420). If it recurs, bound the wait on the pane's own signal as TD-088 did, rather than widening 0.6 s.

**Related:** `tests/README.md` (the fixtures' timing rules), TD-069 (where it was seen).

## TD-080: A manager's round log reads as unpushed work forever

**Priority:** Medium
**Added:** 2026-09-20 (the anchor session; Paul: *we have another 308 unpushed message — that session told me it was caught up; let's check on it*)
**Owner:** anchor
**Kind:** build
**Pickable:** no — part (1) is in the manager's brief, the anchor's
**Status:** Partly done — **part (2), the measure, is built 2026-09-20 (PR #270, a worker):** `GitInfo.unpushed` and `GitInfo.pushed_against`, computed once by the host agent in `gitinfo.py` by §4.2's three rules — the branch's own `origin/<branch>` where it has one, the upstream where it does not, and *on no remote-tracking branch at all* where there is neither — and read by the card's flag, Ready to close (which now says what it measured against when it fails) and `ao team stop --close`, whose own third git test is gone: three tests became one. `ahead` is still on the record beside it and still means *ahead of the upstream*, which is *unmerged*. Part (1), the round log leaving git, goes with the manager's brief in TD-076 and is **not built** — until it is, the manager's worktree reads 0 unpushed by rule 1 (its log is pushed to `origin/<branch>` at wrap-up) rather than 308 by the old measure, so the false row is gone even while the log stays.
**Location:** `docs/briefs/orchestrator-ao-1.md` (round step 4: *append one line per round to `docs/briefs/orchestrator-ao-1.log` … commit it to your launch branch*), `src/agentorc/briefs/lead.md`, `src/sessionorc/gitinfo.py` (`ahead`, read against the branch's upstream), `src/agentorc/teamrun.py` (`_unsafe_to_close`: a third test of *pushed*, folded into the one measure by the design of 2026-09-20), `src/agentorc/ui/app.py` (`ready_to_close`, `state_kind` → the *unpushed* row)

**Why:** the session was right — nothing is stranded. Branch `orchestrator-ao-1` is *ahead 308, behind 173* of **`origin/main`, which is its upstream**, and its whole difference from main is two files: `docs/briefs/orchestrator-ao-1.log` (582 lines) and `orchestrator-ao-1.relayed.json`. The 308 commits are the manager's own round log, one commit per round since run 1, exactly as its brief tells it; they are pushed to `origin/orchestrator-ao-1` at wrap-up (the remote sits at run 5's wrap-up; run 6's rounds are local until this run ends). Because the branch tracks `origin/main`, where those commits can never land, every record in that worktree — the live manager and the exited `…-push` session alike — reads *308 unpushed*, and the Inbox raises *exited with unpushed work* for it, counted, for ever. A row that is always there teaches a person to ignore the row.

**Recommended:** (1) **stop committing a round log to a branch.** It predates what the record now holds — `ao doing` (the round, with its age), the progress and findings channels, the run log — and a 582-line file in git is the least readable of them; the brief writes its one line per round to a file under the home (`runs/`), or nowhere. (2) **Measure *unpushed* against the branch's own remote** (`origin/<branch>`) when it has one, and against the upstream only when it does not — *ahead of main* is *unmerged*, which is a different question from *exists only on this machine*, and the row is about the second. (3) Until then the row for this worktree is noise, and Paul may dismiss it once TD-079's Dismiss exists for state rows.

**Done when** a manager that has run for a week raises no *unpushed* row unless commits exist only on this machine, and the briefs no longer tell it to commit a log.

**Related:** TD-069 (the *unpushed* row), TD-079 (a row that cannot be answered is a row to fix), design §4.2 (Ready to close), TD-076 (the manager's brief is rewritten there anyway).

## TD-083: A worker that ends its run on purpose parks its team

**Priority:** Medium
**Added:** 2026-09-20 (the anchor session; found by `orchestrator-ao-1` at the end of ao-grind's first run and written up in its summary and on the board, PRs #274 and #276)
**Owner:** anchor
**Kind:** live-check
**Pickable:** no — all three steps built; the *done when* is a live run
**Status:** **Step 2 (the chip) built 2026-09-20 (`tdgrind-ao-2`, PR #323):** the **restart wanted** mark on the card and the Focus header, shaped like *out of work* and following its rules — fixed words, the `why` on hover because it is a sentence a card cannot hold, never pressable, not a state, and one malformed record costs that card its chip and not the grid. **It draws `early` differently**, which is the one thing the design did not have to say and the field does: a controller does not act on an early one, so a chip that looked the same would promise a restart that is not coming; it reads *restart wanted · early*, in the needs-you tokens, and its hover says why it is for a person. Partly done — **designed 2026-09-20**, **step 1 built 2026-09-21 (PR #317, `tdgrind-ao-1`)**: `ao progress restart --why` sets `restart_wanted: {at, why, early?}` on the session's own record — declared only by the session it is about, refused without a reason, refused while an outcome is owed to the person (the fresh run does not carry the conversation the debt was made in, so nobody is left who can report it), and refusing `none` as `none` refuses it; a later declared claim clears it, since the session went on after all. `RESTART_EARLY` is thirty minutes: a restart asked inside that of the record's own start still stands — the word is the session's — but carries `early: true`, and a controller puts an early one on the board rather than acting on it. `ao status -v` prints it, and it is in the wake digest, so a lead's `ao wait` returns on it: that is what makes it a thing a manager acts on rather than a field nobody reads. Step (2), the chip, is `tdgrind-ao-2`'s and was blocked on this. **Step (3) built 2026-09-21 (the anchor), after the promote that made the verb live**: `docs/briefs/tdgrind-ao-1.md` (and so `tdgrind-ao-2.md`, which follows it), `docs/briefs/orchestrator-ao-1.md` and the presets `grinder.md`, `hunter.md` and `lead.md` — a worker ends a run with one of the three words and only then the summary; a lead restarts a member that wants it from the record's fields (`early` → the board; suspended → left alone; dirty or unpushed → one send, then the board; else `ao close` and the crash rule's `ao new`, one ceiling), counts it as not finished, and the exemption for *a written end-of-run summary* is gone — the twenty-minute send names the three words. **They take effect at the next team start**; the running `ao-grind` sessions were started on the old briefs. **One gap the briefs showed, left open here:** a hunter whose area is *covered* (quiet, not gone) stops with no word — §4.9a gives it none, `none` would be false and `restart` is not it — so under *declared, never inferred* its lead will ask it; whether *covered* needs a word of its own is a design question, not decided. The design takes the first option whole and half of the second: a third declaration, `ao progress restart --why` → `restart_wanted: {at, why}`, acted on by the controller inside the one restart ceiling; and *a summary is not a declaration* in both briefs. Making a submitted `/exit` an exit was **not** chosen — it has the adapter read what a session typed. Build: (1) the RPC, the field, its refusals (no reason; an outcome owed; `none` already declared), the early-declaration guard (`RESTART_EARLY`, recorded as the sub-field `restart_wanted.early`), `ao status -v`, and `wait` waking the lead on it — tdgrind-ao-1; (2) the chip — tdgrind-ao-2; (3) the two briefs and the three preset briefs, which take effect at the next team start. Not the same gap as TD-053's, which is a worker that *declared* itself out of work.
**Location:** `docs/briefs/orchestrator-ao-1.md` (the round's state rules), `docs/briefs/tdgrind-ao-1.md` (*Shutdown*), `src/sessionorc/agent.py` (`_out_of_work`, the `progress` channel), `src/agentorc/adapters/claude_code/` (what a submitted `/exit` is), design §4.8 and §4.9a

**Why:** 2026-09-20, 18:36 UTC: `tdgrind-ao-1` ended its run deliberately after ten merged PRs — its context was long, the ledger still held pickable entries, and it said a restart with fresh context would pick up cleanly. It could not say so in any way its manager can act on. It was not **out of work**, so `ao progress none` would have been a lie and it rightly did not send it. A Claude Code `/exit` does not leave the session (TD-053 records that), so it was never **`exited`**, and the manager's restart rule fires only on `exited`. It was `idle` with a written end-of-run summary, which the brief says is *not* idle-with-open-work. Closing it is reserved for wrap-up. So the manager put a steer to Paul, the steer lapsed at its bound to its default — park — and the team did nothing for ninety minutes with work on the ledger, until a person acted: the anchor session, on Paul's word, ran `ao team stop ao-grind --close` (about 19:57 UTC; the worker settled and was closed), then closed the manager by hand — its own submitted `/exit` had not left either, so its name was still held — and ran `ao team start ao-grind` at about 20:10 UTC. (That sequence is the anchor session's own record of 2026-09-20; the board PRs #274 and #276 record the parking and the wrap-up, not the restart.)

Two things are missing, and the design round chooses between them or takes both:
1. **A worker cannot say *restart me*.** A third declaration beside `done` and `none` — the run is over, the lane is not — that a manager may act on by closing the record and starting it again under the same name and brief, inside the restart ceiling (three in two hours). It is a structured field, never text the manager parses.
2. **A submitted `/exit` is not an exit.** Either the adapter makes it one (the session is closed when the tool has plainly been told to leave and has settled), or a summary without a declaration does not count as an ending and the manager's idle rule applies to it.

**Done when** a worker that ends its run with work left is back at work, with fresh context, without a person — and one that is truly finished still is never restarted.

**Related:** TD-053 (out of work, §4.9a — the declared case), TD-081 (a same-name start keeps the record's mail: a restart here must too), TD-026 (schedules), TD-076 (the manager's brief is rewritten by the rename).


## TD-091: Nothing says how much context a session has left, or that it has just compacted

**Priority:** Low
**Added:** 2026-09-20 (`tdgrind-ao-1`, from the neighbours survey, TD-059)
**Owner:** anchor
**Kind:** evaluation
**Pickable:** no — endorsed for evaluation, design first, not scheduled
**Status:** Open — recommended by the survey, not approved; design first (§4.5a: a card and Focus field) **2026-09-21, Paul: should grinders (and hunters) compact after each TD?** The anchor's answer, pending research: a session cannot run `/compact` itself, so it is a manager's act (`ao send`), not brief guidance; between TDs a grinder's state is all in the ledger, so a fresh run (`ao progress restart`, TD-083) beats a compaction there, while a hunter's state is what it has explored, so compaction suits it better; per-TD is too often for either; and **the missing piece is this entry's signal** — with the context's size on the record, the rule is a manager's: *at a task boundary, past a threshold, restart a grinder or compact a hunter*. A Sonnet research pass on when compaction pays (cache economics, auto-compact's trigger, what survives, idle time against the cache's lifetime) was started the same day; its findings, and the configuration they suggest, land in this entry. **The research, 2026-09-21 (Sonnet; its sources: Anthropic's prompt-caching, pricing and compaction pages, the Claude Code hooks page, Anthropic's context-engineering post, and four open issues on `anthropics/claude-code`; the arithmetic is the researcher's own model from the published multipliers — about 1,500 tokens added and 500 written per turn — not a measurement):** (1) **only a user or the harness can run `/compact`** — a session cannot (open feature requests ask for it) — and a typed `/compact <instructions>` takes instructions for that one run; (2) a compaction is **an ordinary model call over the whole context**, and what decides its price is whether the cache is warm: a cached read is a tenth of base input, a five-minute cache write 1.25×, a one-hour one 2×, and the cache lives five minutes unless the longer one is asked for; (3) **on a warm cache, compacting beats both carrying on and a fresh start on tokens** — the break-even is about five further turns at 40k of context, two at 100k, fewer beyond — and **a fresh start is never the cheaper one on tokens while the cache is warm** (its re-read of brief and docs is an uncached write, and it lands at a larger context than a summary does): *the anchor's first answer above had this backwards — a restart's case is quality and a clean head, not price*; (4) **idle time turns it round**: past the cache's lifetime the whole context is paid for again at full price on the next turn, so compacting a *cold* 100k context costs about twice a fresh start — **compact before a long wait (CI, a reviewer, the anchor's merge queue), not after it**; (5) quality: Anthropic names *context rot* and advises compacting at a clean boundary, warning that an aggressive summary loses what only later turns out to matter — so a **hunter**, whose state is what it has explored, is compacted at a pause and never restarted mid-investigation, and a **grinder**, whose state is in the ledger, may be either; (6) `PreCompact` and `SessionStart` with `source: compact` are the hooks around it — the second is how state is re-injected after one, and is already read here (TD-090) — and reports disagree on whether `PreCompact` fires for an automatic compaction; the automatic trigger's threshold is set by environment (`CLAUDE_AUTOCOMPACT_PCT_OVERRIDE` and others), its default not documented. **What it suggests as configuration** (per profile, since windows and prices differ): a context threshold past which a session is compacted or restarted *at a task boundary*, and an idle length past which it is compacted *before* the wait; the ordering — boundary, then expected idle, then the raw threshold — is policy, not configuration; the act is its manager's (`ao send "/compact"`, or the restart rule). **What is not observed yet**: this entry's context size and `compacted_at`, and any notion of *about to wait* — the host agent knows a session is idle, not that it is about to be. On a subscription the unit is the usage window rather than dollars; the ratios hold. **Design review 2026-09-22:** endorsed — it is the one thing a person reading a quiet worker cannot see; ledgered for evaluation, not scheduled.
**Location:** `src/agentorc/adapters/claude_code/` (the transcript under `~/.claude/projects/`, which the adapter already reads for the model), the record, the card and Focus header; design §4.2, §4.3 (an optional adapter capability), §4.5a

**Why:** a session near its context limit is about to compact, and one that has just compacted has lost most of what it knew — the two moments a lead reading a quiet worker, or a person deciding whether to trust a long-running session's next step, most wants to know about. agentorc shows a model and each profile's usage windows and nothing about context. `tallu-wonder/agentboss` reads it from the transcript: the context in use is the last non-sidechain usage record's input + cache-creation + cache-read + output tokens, and a `type: "system", subtype: "compact_boundary"` record's `compactMetadata.postTokens` resets it until the next turn ([ADR 2026-09-20](decisions/2026-09-20-session-desk-neighbours.md)). The window size is the weak part: Claude Code's transcript does not state it, so agentboss guesses by model name.

**Fix:** an optional adapter capability, `context()` → `{used, window?, compacted_at?}`, read on the tick by modification time as the model is; the record carries it; the card and Focus show a small gauge (or `compacted 4m ago`), §4.5a row first. A tool that cannot say it shows nothing.

**Done when** a Claude Code session's card shows its context in use, and a compaction is visible on it within a tick.

**Related:** TD-090 (a compaction's `SessionStart`), TD-087 (the usage windows, the other budget), design §4.3.

## TD-092: Nothing reaches a person who is not looking at the page when a session needs them

**Priority:** Low
**Added:** 2026-09-20 (`tdgrind-ao-1`, from the neighbours survey, TD-059)
**Owner:** paul
**Kind:** decision
**Pickable:** no — the channel is Paul's choice
**Status:** Open — recommended by the survey, not approved; the channel is Paul's choice **Design review 2026-09-22 (the anchor, Fable):** endorsed as the cheapest, highest-value item on the ledger — an ntfy topic for what the Inbox counts under *Needs you*; ledgered for evaluation, not scheduled.
**Location:** `src/agentorc/ui/` (the Inbox, the top bar's count), design §4.5, §4.5a

**Why:** the Inbox is where a person works from, and a `needs-you` session or an `ask` sits there until somebody opens the page. Three of the neighbours read in TD-059 push instead: agentboss sends a desktop notification on a transition into *needs you* that jumps to the session; `multi-agent-shogun` and another dashboard send to ntfy / Pushover, and the latter only when the person is not looking at the dashboard. An unattended team whose only question is waiting on a closed browser tab is a team that waits ([ADR 2026-09-20](decisions/2026-09-20-session-desk-neighbours.md)).

**Fix:** decide the channel — web push from the UI (a service worker; no second service, works on a phone), or an ntfy topic in the home's config — and the rule: only for what the Inbox counts under *Needs you*, only on a transition into it, never twice for one row, and not while the person has the page focused. §4.5a gains the opt-in control first.

**Done when** a session going `needs-you` while no page is open reaches the person once, and opening it lands on that row.

**Related:** TD-069 (the Inbox), TD-003 (the phone layout), design §4.5.

## TD-093: Who must look at a PR before it merges is a sentence in a brief and a message in an inbox

**Priority:** Medium
**Added:** 2026-09-21 (the anchor session; asked for by Paul the same day)
**Owner:** anchor
**Kind:** build
**Pickable:** no — slice 3 is PR #479, the anchor's
**Status:** Open — **slice 3 built 2026-09-23 (the anchor, PR #479):** `prs_waiting(members)` in `ui/app.py` sums each record's `prs_waiting: {n, oldest}` into the team header's *n PRs waiting · oldest <age>* (`group_head.html`, display only), and an Inbox `ask` carrying `pr` draws `#<n>` as a link to the PR (`review.pr_url`, from the sender's checkout's `origin`; bare when that cannot be said). **The `org.yml` line is set, 2026-09-23 22:00 MDT** (`review: {reader: techlead, held: ["src/sessionorc/**", "docs/briefs/**"]}` on the grinder role; `ao roles` reads it back), so the anchor's hand rule has ended: the next `ao team start ao-grind` carries it onto the grinders' records. Left: the live look of the header count and the link on the next held PR (Paul's browser walk). Was: **slice 4, the briefs, written 2026-09-23 (the anchor; PR #512):** the techlead preset gains *A held PR* (the gate first, the diff against the design, the reply on the thread with `--source`, the merge itself for an unattended asker and a recommendation for an interactive one), the grinder preset's merge step goes through `ao pr held <n>` (held → `ao msg --kind ask --pr <n> {techlead}`, next item, findings on the thread, the person past the bound), and this repo's three briefs and the techlead primer say the hand is the seat's: `review: {reader: techlead, held: [src/sessionorc/**, docs/briefs/**]}` on the grinder role in `org.yml`, which the anchor sets once this PR is merged and promoted (the record reads it at the next `ao team start`). **The anchor's hand rule ends with that `org.yml` line** — until it is set, `ao pr held` says *not held* and the old briefs' rule no longer exists, so the two land together. Left: slice 3 (#479). Was: **slice 1 built 2026-09-23 (`grinder-ao-1`, PR #477; `src/sessionorc`, the anchor merges):** `review: {reader, held, bound}` on a role preset (`ROLE_KEYS`, checked by `models.normalize_review` in the loader and the host agent alike), carried onto the record, home-owned, by `ao new --role` and `ao team start`; `pr` on a mail entry, `ao msg --pr <n>`, refused on any kind but `ask`; and `prs_waiting: {n, oldest}` on the view. **Left**, with slice (2)'s `ao pr held <n>` built the same day (PR #478): (3) the team header's *n PRs waiting* and the Inbox's `#<n>` link; (4) the briefs. Was: **designed 2026-09-21 (the anchor; design §4.9b *The reader*, §5's example, one §4.5a row), and the shape decided by Paul the same evening: the setting is the role's** (`review: {reader, held, bound}` on a role preset, carried onto the record at start — some teams need it and some do not), **`held:` defaults to every PR**, approval is required for an unattended session's PR and a recommendation for an interactive one's (*much of the time, if the reader recommends a PR two other agents have reviewed, I will accept it*). Also asked: *seat* was not in the glossary — added. The build is unscheduled — (a) a role preset's `review: {reader, held, bound}`, `held` by path globs defaulting to `**`; (b) the techlead is the reader, woken by an `ask` that carries a new integer field `pr` (`ao msg --pr <n>`), it merges a worker's PR itself and only *recommends* on a person's session's PR; (c) the queue is the seat's inbox — `asks_waiting`, the *Waiting on them* row with a link and age, a *PRs waiting* header count; (d) the author picks its next item, rebases its own open held PRs, the reader merges oldest first; (e) agentorc records and shows, GitHub enforces only where the reader has its own account (`CODEOWNERS` mirroring `held:`), the cadence script is untouched. Build order when it comes: the `pr` field and `--pr`, and `review` as a record field set from the preset at start (`src/sessionorc`, the anchor merges), the check of a PR's files against the record's `held:` in the author's own `ao` (a helper, `ao pr held <n>`, for a brief to call), the two rows, the briefs (grinder, hunter, techlead, manager presets; this repo's own). Was: **the rule is decided and in force by hand; the configuration is not designed.** Paul, 2026-09-21: *we might want a configuration to be able to require "techlead merges", so that Fable gets a look at PRs before they merged. We can use that cadence here in the meantime.* **In the meantime, in this repo:** every PR that touches `src/sessionorc` — and the repo's own briefs and `org.yml` — is read and merged by the anchor (the high-trust model), after the author's own independent review; a worker's PR of that kind stops at green CI plus its `cadence-review:` comment. Everything else a worker merges itself on a passing `scripts/check_cadence.py`. **A requirement added by Paul, 2026-09-21: a person's own interactive session inside a team, with the same reader.** *As a user I would like to be able to run an interactive session from within the team, where the techlead would still review merges — a safety net for when I am not intimately familiar with an architecture but want to make changes.* So the rule keys on **the repo and its paths, never on who wrote the PR or what mode their session is in**: a PR from the person's session gets the reader a grinder's does. What exists already: `ao new --team <team>` gives a person's session the team badge, and with it the mail edge to the techlead (§4.10 *sideways*), and an interactive session stays out of every policy's reach (§9 invariant 5). What the design has to add: the techlead **woken by a PR** as it is by a question (a seat filled on demand has no other way to hear of one), and — for the person's own PR — its review as **a recommendation the person may overrule**, never a gate on them. **2026-09-22:** the hand rule is now in all three of this repo's briefs (PR #406 — the manager watches members' PRs on the three paths and asks Paul after two hours; grinder-ao-1's brief had no such line until now, which is how #402 merged without the anchor's read). The build (the role's `review:` block) is still unscheduled.
**Location:** design §4.9b (the techlead), §4.8 (role presets, grants), `.agentorc.yml` / the team definition, the briefs; slice 2 built 2026-09-23 (`grinder-ao-1`, PR #478): `ao pr held <n>` in `src/agentorc/review.py` and `cli.py` (§4.7), which reads the record's `review` and says whether the PR waits for its reader, so (3) the header and the link and (4) the briefs are what is left; `scripts/check_cadence.py` is a SYNCED file (dev-cadence) and is not edited here

**Why:** the rule earns its keep — of the PRs held for the anchor's read on 2026-09-20/21, #301 (a node served `suspend` itself) and #318 (three findings, one of them a false sentence about a host's identity mode) had each already passed the author's cheaper-model review, and #307's review, which came twelve minutes after the anchor's read, did not find what that read had (a restart still polled at once). And the way the rule is carried fails in both directions. It lives in mail and in a brief's prose, so a worker that did not read its inbox between claim and merge merged a `src/sessionorc` PR itself (#343, the doorbell — whose test is TD-094); and it has **no answer to the reviewer being away**: the five PRs of TD-075's mail half sat green for eight hours overnight, the grinder ran out of other work, and the whole team wound down correctly with its work blocked on one reader (the manager said so on the board, twice). A rule that matters this much should be something a team is configured with, shown on the page, and honest about its queue.

**What the design round has to settle:** (a) **where it is said** — a repo's `.agentorc.yml` (it is the repo's policy: *paths* → *who merges*) or the team definition (it is the team's seat that does it), and by path, by label, or for every PR; (b) **who the reader is** — §4.9b's techlead is started per batch and holds no grants, which fits a reader woken by a PR as it is woken by a question, but merging is an act on the repo, not on a session, so agentorc can *record and show* it and cannot enforce it: GitHub's branch protection and CODEOWNERS are where enforcement lives, and the design should say which half is ours; (c) **the queue is a first-class thing** — a PR waiting for its reader is a row somewhere a person sees (the Inbox's *Waiting on them*, the team header), with its age, and **a bound**: past it the PR goes to the person rather than idling a team; (d) **the author is not idle meanwhile** — what a worker may pick while its PR waits, and how stacked PRs are handled (the five above conflicted with each other in `models.py`, the tests and one ledger line, and the reader resolved them after the authors had gone); (e) the cadence check's `review` row is self-attested by the author — a second, reader's line is a change to dev-cadence, not to this repo.

**Done when** the design says where the rule lives and what agentorc does and does not enforce, a team can be configured with it, the waiting PRs and their age are on the page, and a PR past its bound reaches the person.

**Related:** TD-075 (the techlead — the natural reader), TD-083 (the ending that made the overnight stall a clean wind-down rather than a parked team), docs/cadence.md §4 (the independent review), the memory note *read-inbox-before-merging*.

## TD-095: The Org's cards say the same thing several times, are uneven, and *working* does not stand apart

**Priority:** Medium
**Added:** 2026-09-21 (the anchor session; asked for by Paul from a screenshot of the live Org page, kept as `docs/mockups/reviews/2026-09-21-org-cards.png`)
**Owner:** paul
**Kind:** live-check
**Pickable:** no — built; what is left is the live look
**Status:** Built — live look pending (the *done when*'s last clause; on the board). **(f) and (i) built 2026-09-22 (PR #414, grinder-ao-2)**: `view()` draws *unseen* only on a record that is not `unattended`, and the team header sits on the body's 13 px with its title at a card name's 16 px. **(g) and (h) built 2026-09-22 (grinder-ao-2, the PR after #414)**: the techlead preset's label is *Tech Lead* (the other built-ins take their name title-cased already); Claude Code's weekly window is labelled `week`, the chip reads *grind · week 89%*, and a profile with no reading *grind: no reading yet* — the page and `app.js` together, design §4.5a's usage row with them. **Polish, from Paul's look at the live page 2026-09-21 evening (a free-pick with the page):** (g) role labels are titles — *Tech Lead* — so the presets' `label:` (`repoconfig.py`, *Tech lead* today) and the mockups' are title-cased; an auditor's *Auditor · docs* is the label plus the definition's name, and the name stays as written; (h) the top bar's usage chip *wk 89%* is short for no reason and *stale grind no reading* beside it is cryptic: it is the account's weekly window (the label is `Window(label="wk")` in the Claude Code adapter, drawn as `<profile> wk 89%`), so *claude · week 89%*, and *grind: no reading yet* for the dimmed *no reading* chip; (i) the team header's fonts are smaller than the cards' from when it carried less — `.ghead .h2` at 14 px against a card's `.sc .name` at 16 px, and the header container at 12 px in the page's sans while the cards sit on the 13 px body (the `.meta` class is one and the same, 11.5 px mono, on both) — one scale, the header's title no smaller than a card's name. **(f) decided by Paul 2026-09-21: *unseen* is drawn only on an interactive session** (§4.2; a finished worker is plain `idle` — its manager read the result, and the slot says *out of work*); the page still marks unattended sessions, a free-pick with the TD-097 card. **Designed 2026-09-21 with Paul** (design §4.5 *The card's anatomy* and the §4.5a rows it changes; the sketch he chose from is `docs/mockups/reviews/2026-09-21-card-proposal.png`): six fixed rows at one height; a name once; the branch on a row of its own, tool · account · model on every card; one clock; an ending said once per place; the mode as a plain word with its toggle in *more*; the team's header without its manager's card in it; *working* green, *idle* blue, everything ended one grey. **His decisions**: the manager's card stays a card; blue for idle; the slot is two lines for now; a team's profiles are commonly mixed, so the profile line stays on the card; *unattended* is a word, not a chip. **Build order**: (1) the mockups (`docs/mockups/gen.py`), the legend (*States & badges*) with them — the anchor or grinder-ao-2; (2) the page — grinder-ao-2 — and what three review rounds made explicit for it: **the slot's order** (what needs a person or explains a stop → an ending, *out of work* and *restart wanted* among them → the `doing` line → the tail) **and its caption** (*ready to close ✓* lives there); **the buttons move from the slot to the foot**, the first by state — Allow / Deny, Switch profile… / Wait, **Forget for `exited` even when it reads ready to close**, Close session only for an `idle` session that passes, Details, else Focus; *under `<controller>`* ends row 3 and the `stops` note joins row 2; the team's header **loses its manager's name, state and line** (Paul's TD-074 decision of 2026-09-19, reversed by him); the stylesheet's shared rule for idle / exited / unreachable **is split**, `--done` leaves the card, *finished · unseen* stays idle's blue; the *more* entry for the mode; and the pill is the state and nothing else — a session that declared out of work is still `idle`; (3) Paul looks at the live page. One thing left for a later look, noted during the Suspend test: what the suspended mark's `why` should say. As first written: a design session is owed, the anchor's, with Paul: §4.5 and §4.5a first (a card's fields and their order are the design's), then the mockups (`docs/mockups/gen.py`), then the page (grinder-ao-2's lane). Not a free-pick until the design lands. **A second pass, asked for by Paul 2026-09-21 after the design merged (#375) — (a), (b) and (c) are written into the design (§4.5 *The card's anatomy* and its order rule, §4.5a's *mine* and editor-button rows, §5 *The person's own* — `~/.agentorc/ui.yml`, `open_in:`), so the builder builds once; (d) is open:** (a) ***interactive* is the mode that stands out**, since those are the person's own sessions and finding them is a task: *unattended* stays a plain word, *interactive* takes the `person` icon and full-strength text — a mark, never pressable; the order stays urgency first, and **within one urgency an interactive session sorts ahead**; the Org filter gains a one-press *mine*.
